# C H R I S T
## The WAY, the TRUTH,
### and the LIFE.

->*Containing*<-

### How one should use CHRIST for everything, and par-
### ticularly for *Sanctification*.
<note>The term "Sanctification" <term>Heiligmaking</term> refers to the theological process of being made holy and set apart for God through the work of the Holy Spirit.</note>

->*Written in English by*<-

## Mr. JOHN BROWN,
<note>original: "Johannes Brown." This refers to John Brown of Wamphray (c. 1610–1679), a prominent Scottish Presbyterian minister and theologian who lived in exile in the Netherlands.</note>

->Minister of the Holy Gospel.<-

->*And translated by*<-

## JACOBUS KOELMAN,
<note>Jacobus Koelman (1632–1695) was a Dutch minister and a key figure in the "Nadere Reformatie" (Dutch Further Reformation), known for his prolific translations of Scottish and English Puritan works.</note>

->Teacher of the Congregation of Sluis in Flanders.<-

->*To which is added a*<-

->*by the same Author /*<-

->On COLOSS. 1 Verse 27.<-

<meta>This page serves as the preface to a theological work, continuing the introductory material. The translator is addressing the congregation of Sluis and those who participate in 'conventicles' (private religious meetings). It establishes continuity with a previous translation of William Guthrie's *The Christian's Great Interest*.</meta>

# To the Godly
## R E A D E R,

*And in particular to those who gladly wish to hold and attend holy private meetings <note>These were informal gatherings of believers, often called "conventicles" or "gezelschappen," held for prayer and study outside of formal church services.</note>: And most especially, to the Sanctified in the Congregation of Sluis in Flanders.*

->*Dear and Beloved in the Lord:*<-

<image-desc>A large decorative initial letter 'G' within a square frame, featuring intricate foliate designs and scrolls. Inside the curve of the 'G', there appears to be a bird-like figure.</image-desc>

Just as eight years ago I translated and brought to light for you the useful and well-crafted treatise written by a well-tested Scottish Minister, namely, *The Christian’s Great Interest*; so I bring forth to you here a no less precious treatise, written by another excellent Minister of the Church of Scotland. And I may call it, *The Second Part of the Christian’s Great Interest*, because that which was lacking in the first—namely, *using Christ for sanctification in all cases*—is here abundantly handled. Very little has been written on this subject, especially in the Netherlands; indeed, this matter is seldom handled in the pulpits, as the Ministers are often accustomed to occupy themselves with other things; although undoubtedly the foundation of this matter has been preached many times by all Ministers, yet not so clearly and distinctly. As a result, it is found that not only the common professors <term>Believers who have made a public profession of their faith</term>, but even the Godly in the land, and those who are considered the most understanding in the congregations, little understand and grasp and practice this matter—indeed, they proceed therein very darkly and confusedly; so that they can give little account of the basis and manner of their actions, or guide others in this. Nevertheless, I assure myself, and I do not doubt at all, that all tested and earnest Christians will confess and agree with me in this: That these Gospel- <note>The text ends here on a catchword; "Gospel-" (Euangeli-) likely continues as "Gospel-truths" or "Gospel-mysteries" on the following page.</note>

* 2

<summary>In this preface, the translator introduces a new Scottish theological work focused on the practical application of Christ for sanctification, framing it as a necessary sequel to Guthrie's 'The Christian's Great Interest'. He notes that this specific teaching is often overlooked or poorly understood in the Dutch churches of the time.</summary>
<keywords>Sanctification, Sluis, Flanders, Scottish Ministry, William Guthrie, The Christian's Great Interest, private meetings, Reformed piety</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface to the reader. The text picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, completing the thought that even well-regarded believers need a clearer understanding of how to actively apply their faith in Christ to their daily lives.</meta>

# To the Reader.

—the Gospel duty of using Christ intelligently for everything, and in all cases, needs, and difficulties through faith, is the fundamental duty and the mother-duty <note>The author uses "mother-duty" and "mother-grace" to describe the primary source from which all other religious obligations and virtues flow.</note> from which the others flow; just as Faith is the first grace, and the mother-grace, through which the others receive their life, activity, and power. This duty is an exercise of the Great Commandment, and it gives Christ the greatest honor, placing Him in His employment and the execution of His Offices <term>Christ's three roles as Prophet, Priest, and King</term>, and answers the Lord's objective which He has in the Gospel Covenant of Grace—namely, to be acknowledged, served, and glorified through that way of dependency and making use of the Mediator.

This duty is the nearest and most proper means to become fruitful in good works, and to grow and increase in sanctification <term>The process of being made holy</term>, and to be comforted and gladdened by the glorious riches of God’s grace. And therefore, nothing can be more desirable for those who gladly wish to please the Lord and live in close and sweet fellowship with Him—and to that end would consider it their highest happiness if they might significantly grow and increase in the mortification <note>The spiritual practice of "putting to death" sinful nature or desires.</note> of sins and the awakening of grace, walking in the fear of the Lord and the comfort of the Holy Spirit—than to receive a clear discovery of how they shall use the Fountain of all grace and comfort, and thus walk in all well-pleasing to God, bearing fruit.

How this discovery is made in this book, and how this duty and the manner of putting it well into practice is described here, I leave to you yourselves to judge and to recount when you have read the same. If you require my judgment on it, I must confess to you that I find this chief practice of a Christian described therein in an incomparably clear and concise manner, such that I have never seen or read the like on this matter. I have gladly spent my time translating this into our language, even before it was printed in the English language, so that this precious jewel—

<meta>The text breaks here with the catchword "ge-", likely beginning "gegeven" (given) or "gebracht" (brought) on the following page.</meta>

<summary>The translator explains that "using Christ" through faith is the primary "mother-duty" that enables all other Christian virtues and growth in holiness. He recommends this specific book for its unique clarity on how to practically apply this dependency on Christ in everyday life.</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, completing the sentence from the previous page regarding how the knowledge of "making use of Christ" should be shared. It focuses on the benefit for both ministers and private believers in the Netherlands.</meta>

### To the Reader.

...would become common to many; and I expect, under the Lord’s blessing, much fruit from it. Even Ministers <term>Leeraars — literally "teachers," referring to the clergy</term> will be able to derive uncommon profit from it, in order to learn to preach the Gospel purely, who now often, out of lack of understanding and inexperience, teach people to proceed in a legalistic <term>Wettische — a way focused on following the Law and human effort rather than relying on God's grace</term> manner, as they themselves frequently preach the Gospel in a legalistic way. 

But my primary objective has been to share this noble writing with the private godly souls in the Netherlands, so that those who do not hear these things publicly—or at least hear them seldom and in a more obscured way—may find them here abundantly for their instruction and refreshment. This is so that they might not remain stuck in their doubts, discouragements, and powerlessness, but that they might learn with joy to draw the Water of grace from the Fountain of salvation <note>original: "Fonteine des heils"</note>, and so run with an enlarged, lively heart on the path of God’s commandments. 

But because I know that not all are equally capable of helping themselves with books, it is my earnest request to all who are more gifted and graced, and who through this means shall be made more capable of confirming themselves and others in the course of godliness <term>Godtfaligheidt — a life of sincere piety and devotion</term> and the life of Faith, that they would do their utmost to show these thoughts and methods, indicated here, to others who are weaker, and to convey these ideas to them. This is so that the glory and riches of the Gospel, and the unsearchable Treasures of Christ <term>Schatten Christi</term>, might become better known. 

And therefore, I would wish from the heart that private holy gatherings <term>t’Samenkomsten — informal religious meetings or "conventicles" held in homes outside of official church services</term> might be held more than before. And you, who are inclined to hold and attend such meetings, I want to earnestly stir you up, so that you not only continue in them, but also attract and urge others toward them; and that neither you nor others let yourselves be deterred by the opposition which some, with a semblance of holy zeal, make against them. I speak of such gatherings...

<summary>The author explains that this book is intended to help both ministers and laypeople avoid legalistic thinking and instead find refreshment in the Gospel. He also strongly encourages the practice of holding private religious gatherings in homes to discuss these spiritual methods.</summary>
<keywords>Gospel preaching, legalism, Netherlands, Fountain of salvation, godliness, unsearchable riches of Christ, religious gatherings, conventicles</keywords>

### To the Reader.

<sig>E</sig>

and Gatherings, so that I may be well understood, not wherein one gossips <note>original: "achterklapt"</note> about one's neighbors, or slanders good Teachers <term>Leeraaren — ministers or clergy</term>, despises one's neighbors, speaks vainly or unedifyingly, or incites and lures others toward schism and the leaving of the Church; But where one prays with each other to the Lord, sometimes also with fasting; and where one reverently sings songs of praise to God, and Psalms, and spiritual songs, repeats and applies the preached Word, reads God's Word, and speaks of it, brings up and lays open the various experiences <term>ondervindingen — personal spiritual trials, feelings, or "dealings" with God</term> of the Pious <term>Vroome — the godly or devout</term> in the course and practice of godliness <term>Godsaligheidt — a life of sincere piety and devotion</term>, proposes, handles, and solves cases of conscience <term>gevallen van conscientie — ethical or spiritual dilemmas</term>, and useful questions about certain divine truths—those truths, and in so far as they have regard and influence on the practice of godliness; and whatever more is of such a nature, whether a Minister <term>Predikant</term> be present or not present. And to further arm You <note>original: "U. L." — short for "Uwe Liefden," an affectionate 17th-century formal address meaning "Your Loves"</note> against those who are accustomed to speak against these holy gatherings <term>t'Samenkomsten — small group meetings outside of formal church services</term> bitterly and mockingly, I would give You these reasons for the same, which now occur to me.

1. *Reason*, Because it is the Apostle's command, *Let us not neglect our mutual gatherings, but let us exhort one another*, Hebrews 10: verse 25. Thus he also makes it known there, that one should not neglect, but rather use the Christian gatherings of private Christians, for mutual conversation and the stirring up of one another; it is true, he does not exclude here the public gatherings, but includes them therein, yet that also here particularly the private gatherings are meant and intended, appears from the fact that he wants them to be observed frequently, so that one *should exhort one another*, one private Christian the other; it is then a command of God, under which all Christians lie, which they must obey for conscience's sake, whatever people may say against it.

2. *Reason*, Because the Apostle at the same place considers it as a blemish and a blameworthy fault, that many...

## To the Reader.

...withdrew themselves, and made a habit of withdrawing from those Holy Gatherings <term>H. t’Samenkomsten — private religious meetings or "conventicles" held outside of formal church services</term>, saying, *Let us not do so, as some have the habit*; so then it is a notable evil to separate oneself frequently, not only from the public church Assembly, but also from the private Christian companies of the Believers <term>Geloovige</term>; and the failings of others in this regard should be so far from making us weak or negligent, that it should much rather awaken us to more diligence, to follow such a good means of edification <term>stichtingh — the process of "building up" one's faith or moral character</term>, to the end that we may give others a good example of edification in this.

3: Reason, Because observing such Holy Assemblies <term>H. Vergaderingen</term> is a means to hold fast, to confess, and not to abandon in times of trial the doctrine of Christ and the truth received and believed from Him; and on the contrary, to abandon them is the way toward division <note>original: "scheuringh" — often implying a schism or tear in the church body</note> and apostasy from the truth. For the Apostle <note>The author refers to the writer of the Epistle to the Hebrews</note> prescribes this means of holding and attending mutual gatherings in order to be steadfast in the Holy Confession <term>H. Belijdenis</term>; for that was his exhortation in *verse 23: Let us hold fast the unwavering profession of hope*, and for that purpose he prescribes two means: 1. To look after one another to the sharpening and awakening of each other, *verse 24.* 2. Not to abandon or neglect the mutual gathering, *verse 25.* So that the right way to maintain unity in the Church and in the doctrine of truth is to meet together privately and frequently for mutual admonition, yet without neglect or hindrance of the public assemblies, which, as I said, are also included <note>original: "ingewonden" — literally "wrapped in" or "enwrapped," meaning these duties are inextricably linked</note>.

4. Reason, Because the day of God’s judgment <term>Godts oordeel</term>, particularly over us and generally over all, is at the door; so we should then use this means to make ourselves more fit for our duties, through which we may be in a better state on the day of our death and of our accounting; The Apostle gives this reason in the same place, saying, *Let us not [neglect] the mutual gatherings*

### To the Reader.

...abandon, and that so much the more as we see that the day approaches; He means to say, the observation of this—that there is but a short time between, and our day will have come, and our Lord will come to judgment—should serve as a suitable means and motive to sharpen you toward the observance of such an opportunity, whereby you might be confirmed and encouraged to maintain the truth against all fears of men.

5. Reason, Because Christians are commanded, in *Heb. 10: verse 24, To take heed of one another, to the sharpening of love and of good works.* Which necessarily contains within itself their fellowship with one another, and their frequent gathering with one another, and not only publicly for the open Religious Services (for then we cannot so regard one another for sharpening, since we then, and thereby, cannot see each other's condition <note>original: "gestalte" — meaning one's spiritual state or inner frame of mind</note>, gifts, virtues, etc.), but especially for private Holy Conversation <term>H. t’Samenspraak — spiritual dialogue or conference between believers</term> and interaction; for this regarding of one another consists in this: that we pay attention to one another's disposition, abilities and experiences, virtues and faults, to the end that we may make ourselves more capable of doing good to one another, and of receiving good from one another, and to stir each other up to a holy contest, as to who shall be the first in love and well-doing. Therefore, every Christian is bound to observe the Holy private Gatherings.

6. Reason, Because it is commanded of us that we should exhort one another, *Hebr. 10: verse 25.* Yea, that we should exhort one another every day, *as long as it is called Today, so that no one among us be hardened through the deceitfulness of sin, Heb. 3: verse 13.* That is, everyone must—beyond the fact that the Ministers <term>Predikanten — ordained clergy</term> exhort and awaken the Congregation <term>Gemeinte</term> publicly—speak to their fellow Christians, rouse them, and spur them on toward good, and point out to them the deceptiveness of sin, and seek to prevent the hardness of their hearts, and confirm them in the truth and steadfast...

<meta>This page continues the sixth theological reason for private religious gatherings, specifically focusing on the warning from Hebrews regarding the "evil heart of unbelief." It transitions into the seventh and eighth reasons, citing the Epistles to the Thessalonians to emphasize mutual comfort and spiritual edification.</meta>

### To the Reader.

...confession of religion, as it stands in the preceding twelfth Verse, *Take heed, Brethren, lest there be in any of you an evil heart of unbelief, in departing from the living God*, and then follows, *But exhort one another, etc.* So this is then a necessary and general duty, which is a necessary means to keep people from apostasy <note>falling away from one's faith</note>, and a duty that must be practiced daily, while it is called Today; that is, as often and as long as God gives a present and suitable opportunity for it, so that through neglect of this, the misfortune does not follow that someone would be hardened through the deceitfulness of sin. Now this duty cannot be done, if one does not speak together, and for that end gather together.

7. *Reason*, Because it is commanded that Christians should bring comforting words to one another, and speak of the lovely promises, great privileges, and glorious expectation which the Pious <note>original: "Vroome" — referring to the godly or devout believers</note> have; *So then, says the Apostle, 1 Thess. 4: verse 18. Comfort one another with these words.* Now this cannot happen unless they come together, and speak for that purpose with one another, and pray that the Lord God would bless the means, and bring the words of comfort to the hearts.

8. *Reason*, Because Christians are charged to build up, further, and edify one another's souls, as the Apostle says, *1 Thess. 5: verse 11. Wherefore exhort one another, and edify one another, even as also ye do*: This duty of edifying one another contains a great deal; each must help the other, lend a hand, strengthen, and encourage in and toward the work of the Lord. Now this is impossible to do if one only comes together in the public Assemblies <note>formal church services</note>, and then continues to live as strangers toward one another; it then necessarily follows that the Pious should frequently come together and gather for that purpose.

9. *Reason*, Because it is a general command of the Apostle, *Teach and exhort one another, and speak among...*

### To the Reader.

*...one another with Psalms, and Hymns, and spiritual Songs, singing and making melody to the Lord with grace in your heart*, Col. 3: verse 16. Eph. 5: verse 19. No one can think that this can happen, unless for that purpose people gather together outside of the public Assemblies.

10. *Reason*, Because it flows from the nature of the Christian communion of the Saints <note>The "communion of saints" is a core doctrine in the Apostles' Creed, referring to the spiritual union and mutual service of all believers</note>, which everyone professes to believe; now that communion requires and contains, among other things, a communal sharing of gifts and graces, for the improvement, enlightenment, awakening, and strengthening of one another, which our Catechism <note>Refers to the Heidelberg Catechism, a standard Protestant instructional document used in the Dutch Reformed Church</note> expresses thus in the 55th *Question*, saying, that *everyone, by virtue of the communion of the Saints, must know himself bound to employ his gifts for the use and salvation of the other members, willingly and with joy*; Thus these Holy private Gatherings and meetings must be held, or the communion of the Saints will be denied in practice, and the Catechism contradicted; for how can that sharing and employing of talents for others happen, without often coming together?

11. *Reason*, Because it is commanded, that *one shall confess sins to one another, and pray for one another*, James 5: verse 16. So then one must come to the other, and speak of the matters of the Soul, and be together in prayer, not only in public Assemblies, but privately, private Christians with one another; the more so because the Pattern of prayers <note>The Lord's Prayer</note> shows us that the Believers must frequently worship their heavenly Father in unison, saying, *Our Father*, etc.

12. *Reason*, Because every Believer must conduct himself as a Distributor <note>original: "Uitdeelder" — meaning a steward or someone who dispenses God's grace to others</note>, and not as an Owner and Master of his gifts; since God has given everyone gifts out of grace, for a common use, for the edification of others, according to the encouragement and the charge of the Apostle Peter, 1 Pet. 4: 10. *As every man hath received the gift, even so minister the same one to another, as good stewards of the manifold grace of God*; Everyone must put his talent to use <note>original: "op woeker" — literally "at interest," referring to the Parable of the Talents where servants are expected to grow what was entrusted to them</note>

<meta>Catchword: zet-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that private religious gatherings are essential for practicing the "communion of saints," fulfilling the duty to share spiritual gifts, and obeying biblical commands for mutual confession and prayer.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the twelfth reason for holding private religious gatherings, focusing on the duty of the converted to win souls and employ their spiritual gifts. It then introduces the thirteenth reason, emphasizing the mutual necessity of believers as members of one spiritual body who require each other's support and shared experiences.</meta>

### To the Reader.

...to put <note>Completing the thought from the previous page: "to put [one's talents] to work"</note> to use, seeking to gain profit for his Lord and Master, laboring to catch souls and win them for Christ, and to convert sinners from the error of their ways and save them from death: everyone, having been converted, must also strengthen his brother; everyone must employ the gifts according to the grace given to him, according to *Matt. 25: verses 26, 27; Prov. 11: verse 30; James 5: verse 20; Luke 22: verse 32; Rom. 12: verses 6, 7; 1 Cor. 12: verse 7.* This indicates that it is the Lord's will and pleasure that Christians should hold mutual meetings frequently and on all good occasions.

13. *Reason*, Because the need of Christians requires that they be together frequently, since they have need of one another for help, encouragement, and support; no Christian can so exist on his own that he does not need his neighbor: they must join together, and each fulfill the other's need, and strengthen and support one another’s hands. *Two are better than one, says the Preacher <note>Referring to the author of the book of Ecclesiastes</note>: because they have a good reward for their labor. For if they fall, the one will lift up his fellow; but woe to him that is alone when he falleth; for he hath not another to help him up. And if one prevail against him, then two shall withstand him; and a threefold cord is not quickly broken. Eccl. 4: verses 9, 10, 12.* The members of the mystical Body of Christ <note>A theological concept describing the spiritual unity of all believers with Jesus as the "head"</note> need each other and must have the same care for one another: *the eye cannot say unto the hand, I have no need of thee; the members which seem to be more feeble are necessary, 1 Cor. 12: verses 21, 22, 25.* The <term>spiritual experiences</term> <note>original: "bevindingen" – a central term in Dutch Pietism referring to the internal, lived reality of faith and God's work in the soul</note>, deliverances, and comforts of one are useful for the other. Paul, an Apostle, not only needed the prayers of the believers in Rome, but also the comfort and strengthening through their faith, and for that reason he longed to see them and come to them, *Rom. 15: verse 30; Rom. 1: verses 11, 12, 13.* and in general the Apostle says to the believers...

->To the Reader.<-

*Thessalonians, We pray you, Brothers, exhort one
another, exhort the unruly, comfort the faint-hearted, sup-
port the weak, be patient toward all, 1 Thess. 5:
verses 11, 14.* So the need of the Believer and the Un-
believer calls for Christians to offer spiritual help
to one another through mutual dealings and in
gatherings.

14. *Reason*, Because the recounting of Sermons <note>The historical practice of "repetition," where believers met after a church service to discuss and memorize the minister's sermon</note> is a
duty that not only members of a household should perform
within their home, but also neighbors with one
another, for the remembrance of those who are weak of
memory, and for the help and instruction of
those who could not be present; for
thus says the Lord through the Prophet, *Jerem. 23: 35.*
*Thus shall you say, every one to his Neighbor, and
every one to his Brother; what has the Lord answered?
And what has the Lord spoken?* Which is all the
more necessary because many cannot enjoy
Home-exercises <note>Private family worship or study sessions</note>, and thus receive no spiritual sharing
of gifts, nor a recounting of the preached Word within the
house; and many, moreover, cannot read,
and are very unlearned <note>The original Dutch 'overvaren' suggests being bypassed or lacking depth in the knowledge of the Word</note> in the Word of righteous-
ness; and others, due to some long-lasting illness, or
another evil that befalls them or their household,
are kept at home; The nature of this matter, and those
frequent opportunities and inconveniences, speak
clearly that such spiritual Conversations and communal
Prayers, and therefore holy Gatherings, must be
maintained privately; just as the two Dis-
ciples went to recount to the other Apostles *what they
had seen and heard of the Lord Jesus, Luke 24:
verse 35.*

15. *Reason*, Because it is foretold of the days of
the New Testament, when *God would cause his Servant the Branch <note>A Messianic title for Jesus Christ</note>
to come, that then every one would invite his Neighbor
under the Vine, and under the Fig tree, Zech.
3: verses 8, 10.* That is, because of spiritual Peace,

->To the Reader.<-

## To the Reader.

...all be subject to one another, and that in the fear of God, *1 Peter 5: verse 5; Ephesians 5: verse 21.* And therefore serve one another, and rebuke and receive rebukes from the other; how would this happen, if we did not more often come together?

17. **Reason**, Because Christians, as Brothers and Sisters, Housemates, and Children of one Father, must maintain friendship; they are very closely related and must treat one another with fervent brotherly love, and be kind, as Peter says, *1 Peter 3: verse 8.* So they must come together more often, and visit one another, and speak and write to one another, and associate with one another as intimate Friends (just as the Virgin *Mary* visited her cousin *Elizabeth* for that purpose, *Luke 1: 39-57*) quite differently than they associate with godless worldly people. In the public Gatherings the Godless and the Pious are mixed together, except in the Lord's Supper <note>original: "Avontmaal"; referring to Holy Communion, where traditionally only the faithful were admitted</note>, where only Friends are commanded to eat; therefore other opportunities should be seized, in which they, as one heart and soul, would practice friendship together. It is known that the nature of honest friendship requires social interaction, visiting, conversation, sharing, and hospitality; for its definition is that it is a mutual goodwill which one does not allow to remain hidden. Shall then Christian and Godly friendship not much more have these expressions within it, and require the company and inward fellowship of one with another? Would they then not receive and embrace one another with affection according to the Apostle’s lesson, *Romans 15: verse 7: Receive ye one another, as Christ also received us to the glory of God.*

18. **Reason**, Because it is the Lord’s commandment that people be of one mind, and speak with one language concerning the Holy truth and Godliness; Paul prayed for the Romans thus: *Now the God of patience and consolation grant you to be likeminded one toward another according to Christ* <meta>catchword: Iesum [Jesus]</meta>

## To the Reader.

...Jesus, so that you may with one accord and with one mouth glorify the God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, *Romans 15: v. 5, 6.* <note>The original text cites Romans 5, but the content refers to Romans 15:5-6.</note> And to those of Corinth he said, *I beseech you, Brothers, by the name of our Lord Jesus Christ, that you all speak the same thing, and that there be no divisions among you, but that you be joined together in the same mind, and in the same judgment, 1 Cor. 1: verse 10.* And to the Philippians he says, *You fulfill my joy that you may be like-minded, having the same love, being of one accord, and of one mind, Philipp. 2: verse 2.* And elsewhere we are often commanded to arrange things so that we may be like-minded, as in *1 Cor. 13: verse 11; Philipp. 3: v. 16; 1 Pet. 3: verse 8; Rom. 12: verse 16.* It is therefore a necessary requirement that we frequently come together; for how would we speak the same things and have the same feelings if we were not more often together, and handled spiritual matters familiarly with one another? How can we be of one mind and mouth if we do not share our feelings with one another and express the understanding that we have of these matters; and if there is a difference somewhere, how shall we become like-minded if we do not pray to God together for light and clarity, and to be led into all truth? Therefore, the holy private gatherings of the <term>Belijders</term> <note>Literally "Confessors"; these are people who have made a public profession of their faith.</note> should be beyond dispute and contradiction.

## To the Reader.

...practice has always been praised, promoted, and protected by pious men against those who have opposed it.

22. *Reason*: Because the fruit of such *Holy Gatherings* and mutual interactions has been found to be glorious and abundant through *God's* rich blessing; just as they also, in and of themselves (when observed in simplicity and according to *God's Word*), tend toward and lead to such an end; obedience to *God's* laws is thereby advanced in every way, and *Christ's* Kingdom is furthered by it; just as Christian *House-exercises* <note>Referring to "huis-oeffeningen," the practice of family worship or devotions held within the home.</note> bring exceptionally good profit, so these [private gatherings] also serve directly and naturally to greatly increase the glory of *God* and the edification of souls; Through these private *Exercises* <note>The Dutch term "oeffeningen" refers to "godsdienstoefeningen"—gatherings for prayer, Bible study, and spiritual discussion outside of formal church services.</note> and *Meetings*, the hunger for *the Word* is further sharpened, so that one becomes much more desirous of the ministry of *the Word*, as the spiritual and unadulterated milk, so that one may grow thereby; and it is so far from the truth that these would be detrimental to public *Worship Services*, or would cause them to be despised, that on the contrary, there is hardly a more powerful means to maintain the proper respect for the public means [of grace], and to make the public *Exercises* more fruitful, than these private *Exercises*, when they are managed and observed with proper discretion according to the *Word*; for they serve to apply more closely and bring to the souls that which was presented publicly, and to maintain and awaken the spiritual appetite for more, by which it is then clearly visible that those who frequent these private gatherings are the most conscientious in attending the public *Worship Services*, and that they thrive and grow best through public preaching, more than all those who neglect and despise these same gatherings. And there are none who respect and honor faithful *Teachers* <note>The original "Leeraars" refers to ordained ministers or those authorized to teach the doctrine.</note> more than those who most often apply the *Word* for themselves and for others on every occasion; although they are also accustomed to let the esteem of such *Ministers*...

# To the Reader.

** 2

...[those] who show themselves to be enemies of true piety, and of the power of godliness (of which, alas, there are all too many); but they have only themselves to blame. For the Lord has said, *“You have turned from the way, you have caused many to stumble in the Law; therefore I have also made you despicable and unworthy before all the people; those who honor me, I will honor, and those who despise me shall be lightly esteemed,”* Malachi 2: verses 8, 9; 1 Samuel 2: verse 30.

Furthermore, it is evident that individual souls receive remarkably much good through this practice; for knowledge grows uncommonly because of it: For *“iron sharpens iron, so a man sharpens the countenance of his neighbor,”* says Solomon, Proverbs 27: verse 17. Experience teaches this abundantly, so much so that the enemies of these gatherings and exercises <note>The term "exercises" (Dutch: *oeffeningen*) refers to informal religious meetings where laypeople discussed scripture and shared spiritual experiences.</note> are accustomed to object, among other things, that people become "too wise," or "know-it-alls" <note>Original: *neuswijs*. A Dutch term for someone who is overly inquisitive or considers themselves more learned than they are.</note> as they call it—proud and puffed up by their knowledge (much like the Papists <note>A historical, often derogatory term for Roman Catholics, used here by the Protestant author to criticize religious pride.</note>).

Furthermore, many are converted through this means; indeed, it is experienced in some places that where these exercises are maintained with earnestness, zeal, and spiritual composure, more souls are converted than are observed to be touched by public preaching <note>Formal sermons delivered by a minister during a church service.</note>. Many a soul has felt the first breathings and stirrings of God's Spirit there; there their eyes were first opened, so that they saw that Christianity was something other than mere confession <note>Publicly stating one's faith or adherence to a creed.</note>, church-going, and civic morality. They found there that the doctrine of the Holy Gospel had brought forth a wonderful change and new life in some people; so that through those exercises, they have come not only to a healthy suspicion of themselves and their own spiritual state, but also to the beginnings of spiritual life and renewal. Through this, many have advanced in the art and skill of praying; people who previously might have had enough grace to sigh somewhat to God according to their need, but from hearing the exercise...

# To the Reader.

...[by] the gift of prayer in others, [they] have learned in a short time, according to all circumstances, to express themselves with much pleading and reasoning before God, which they had not learned in such a way through public prayers. Through this, many despairing souls have received counsel on how to conduct themselves well and to act with understanding; many troubled, doubtful, and conflicted souls have found their objections, obstacles, and doubts resolved, answered, and taken away there; many darkened souls, who could not judge how things stood with them, received light and opening there through the account of the experiences of others, and their eyes have been cleared; many dejected, sad, and burdened souls, coming to such private and communal exercises <term>oeffeningen: informal gatherings where laypeople discussed scripture and shared spiritual experiences</term> and conversation, have returned gladdened and well-comforted, and have thanked God that the Lord caused them to meet such a singular word of comfort there; many cold and sluggish hearts have experienced to their wonder that their hearts were warmed, awakened, and set aflame in those particular discussions, so that they might well have said to one another, along with the two travelers to Emmaus <note>A reference to Luke 24:32, where two disciples encounter the resurrected Jesus on the road to Emmaus and later realize their hearts burned with conviction during the talk.</note>:
> Did not our hearts burn within us, while
> we were there together, and sang together, and spoke,
> and prayed, or heard others speak in such a way?
for the Lord Jesus was present there, although at first they had not believed or noticed it. It is there that many sluggish and half-fainting souls were wonderfully encouraged, heartened, and stirred up, so that they rose up holily in the ways of the Lord, so that limp hands and weak knees were strengthened and raised up, and they were singularly spurred on to take up the struggle with new courage, and to withstand and overcome temptations and corruptions. Experience teaches this—a schoolmistress even to fools—so that it is in vain to deny it; taking the test thereof, and hearing out the trials of others, shall [convince] us of the truth...

23. *Reason*: Because it not only cannot be judged unlawful, but is useful and in some respects necessary for people to sometimes come together for civil purposes, it should be judged much less an evil that people come together for spiritual and holy purposes, to stir one another up to godliness <term>Godsaligheid: a life of devotion and reverence toward God</term>, and to guide and advance one another on the path to reach the desired rest. # To the Reader.

...mutual help and the glorification of God, seeing as they have such a great work to do in this World, which is a thousand times weightier than if they were dealing with a worldly Kingdom? Is it not right that Believers <term>Belijders: those who publicly profess their faith</term> should come together more often—those who know and take to heart that within this short and uncertain time they must work out their eternal Salvation; that after a few hours or days they must appear before the righteous and strict judgment of God; that they must soon lay aside this earthly Tabernacle <note>A common biblical metaphor for the physical body, emphasizing its temporary nature</note>, and dying, depart from time and pass into Eternity; that in this life they must make their peace with God, and obtain forgiveness and reconciliation and rest for the Conscience through Christ's Blood; that they must demonstrate a living, powerful faith in Christ, and a healthy and sincere penance and conversion; that they must see their corruptions and sins brought under subjection, and express the Image of God and the renewal of nature? Is it any wonder that such people seek help and counsel and guidance from one another, since they know that the great mass of Professors <note>original: "Belijders"; here referring to those who claim faith in name only</note> deceive themselves with delusions and will remain outside of Heaven, and that they themselves have a deceitful and deadly heart, just like others, and that they as well as others have their temptations and obstacles from within and without? It seems to me rather a great wonder that Christians—who together as travelers and strangers on earth and Citizens of Heaven set their course heavenward, and fight the same fight to be crowned, and carry on the same heavenly business—could be occupied in any other business, or in any other company where one speaks of earthly trifles.

24. *Reason*, Because now is a time in which such private dealings and gatherings of Believers are especially required; for it is *an evil time*, in which iniquity has greatly multiplied, and the love of many has grown cold; *A time*,

<summary>This page argues that the gravity of eternal salvation and the brevity of life make spiritual fellowship a necessity rather than a choice. It emphasizes that true Christians, as "strangers on earth," should naturally prefer heavenly conversation over worldly trifles, especially in a time of declining faith.</summary>

# To the Reader.

** 4

in which offenses and temptations are very many, and Atheism <note>original: "Atheistery"; here referring to a general rejection of God's authority or presence in public life</note> is greatly breaking in and breaking through; and therefore it is all the more timely that the Pious <term>Vroome: the truly devout or godly</term> come together to strengthen one another’s hands and to encourage each other; for this reason it is written so emphatically in Malachi 3:16: *Then those who feared the Lord spoke every one to his neighbor; then*, when the professors <note>Those who publicly claim to be Christians</note> said: *It is in vain to serve God*, and *we consider the proud happy*, etc. It is also a time *of God’s wrath over Land and Church* because of the high-climbing, unlamented, and provocative sins from which the inhabitants of the Land—great and small, leaders and others—have not been willing to turn even unto this day; so that even heavier judgments are to be expected. This is why the Sincere <note>The "Oprechte," or those whose faith is genuine</note> should meet more often to sigh and pray together, and to seek mercy for Ministers, Elders, and Believers, for Magistrates and Subjects, so that they might finally take in hand the work of Reformation <note>The ongoing process of purifying the church and society according to biblical standards</note>, which many have been called to but few have done anything for, before the Lord’s wrath rises against this people until there is no healing. It is a time *in which the spiritual duties of the communion of Saints have become so unknown*, as if each might live like a *Cain*, without being his brother’s keeper, or like a *Gallio* <note>A reference to Acts 18:17; Gallio was a Roman official who "cared for none of these things," here symbolizing religious indifference</note>, without tenderly concerning himself with the affairs of the Church. Thus, these duties of Christian fellowship ought all the more to be practiced by us in an exemplary way—duties which, as I showed above, could not well be performed without private gatherings. It is *a time* in which Holy Home-exercises, Home-prayers, Home-Catechizing <term>Huys-Catecheſatien: the practice of teaching religious principles to children and servants at home</term>, and home-repetitions <note>Reviewing the Sunday sermon within the family circle</note>, etc., are very little found among Believers; indeed, even in the houses of Ministers and Elders of the Congregations, these are for the most part neglected. Therefore, those Gatherings are all the more useful and necessary, not only to fill that void, but to make many capable and willing for such Household Devotions. It is *a time* in which

# To the Reader.

in which there are many incompetent Teachers, who do not know how to handle the Congregation correctly, nor how to bring the Word of the Law and of the *Gospel* <note>original: "Euangeliums"; the core message of Christian salvation</note> to their understanding, and so to apply the Word fittingly to their *Consciences* <note>original: "Conscientien"</note>; so that the help of sensible, tested, and graced Members can contribute much good here. Many Teachers, being merely **formula-prayers** <term>Formulierbidders: ministers who only read pre-written prayers from a service book rather than praying spontaneously from the heart</term>, do not teach the Congregation by example to pray from the Spirit; so that those gatherings, in which one prays together according to all occasions and difficulties, provide a commendable and desirable help in this regard. 

Many Teachers also, even if they might possess some ability, are very sluggish, unwilling, and disinclined to offer a hand to the Believers regarding their private **spiritual state** <note>original: "Zieleftaat"; one's personal condition of faith and relationship with God</note>; they say, "they must do that themselves, and we must commend that to God," as if they did enough simply by preaching publicly. For this reason, those private discussions of the **cases of conscience** <note>Refers to "casuistry," the practice of resolving specific moral or spiritual dilemmas</note> and of the experiences of the Pious, and the resolutions of *scruples* <note>original: "scrupelen"; doubts or hesitations of the soul</note> and doubts in those *private* assemblies, are exceptionally useful and necessary. This is especially true in large cities, where only a few Teachers are appointed as Shepherds over thousands of souls, who then barely address the Sheep with a passing word three or four times a year (and even then, it is always the same thing and does not touch the soul). 

Besides this, it is also *a time* in which the Glory of the Lord has noticeably departed from the Sanctuary, and the Spirit of the Lord has uniquely withdrawn Himself from the ministry of the Word and of the *Sacraments*; so that it is a common experience that the blessing and fruit of the public means is quite small, for many reasons that would be untimely to investigate and bring up here. On the contrary, the experience of the pious teaches that the Lord uniquely blesses private interactions in the holy meetings, which is why *Satan* rises up more hostiley against these than against public assemblies; but that gives us another Reason, namely:

# To the Reader.

25th Reason: Because it is evident and public in every way that these **Holy Gatherings**—even though they are neglected by many due to a lack of the power of **Godliness** <note>original: "Godsaligheidt"; true spiritual devotion rather than just outward ritual</note>—nevertheless, few as they are, are greatly opposed and slanderously denounced by worldly men and hypocritical **Nominal Christians** <note>original: "Naamchristenen"; people who are Christian in name only but do not live according to the faith</note>. In this opposition, the malice, hatred, and envy of Satan uniquely manifest themselves, as he cannot endure this useful means for promoting the growth of Christ’s Kingdom. Some authorities, indeed even the provincial **States** <note>Refers to the "Staten van Lande," the governing bodies of the Dutch provinces</note>, have opposed and forbidden them with public ordinances. People dare to call them by a pagan name, *Conventicles* <term>Conventiculen: small, unauthorized religious assemblies held outside the official state church; the author notes that this term was originally used by pagans to disparage early Christian meetings</term>; for thus the pagans used to name the gatherings of the Christians, making laws against them as illicit societies. 

Many **Teachers** <note>original: "Leeraaren"; referring to ordained ministers</note> are found not only failing to encourage the **Confessors** <note>Believers who publicly profess their faith</note> to hold such holy meetings and private exercises with one another, but also warning against them and speaking contemptuously of them, even from the pulpit. Meanwhile, these same ministers make no difficulty about attending worldly societies and gatherings established for the gratification of the flesh. Indeed, instead of attending those Holy Assemblies themselves sometimes, it is found that they blacken their reputation and combat them, and also mark and brand as semi-sectarians those who gladly take part in them. They report them and denounce them to the **Consistory** <term>Kerkenraadt: the local governing body of the Reformed church, made up of the minister and elders</term>, and sometimes even to the secular authorities. 

I will not recount what recently happened in this regard in **Kruiningen** <note>original: "Cruningen"; a village in the province of Zeeland</note>, a village in the territory of **Goes**, yet it is such that one cannot speak of it without indignation and loathing. Some **Consistories**, **Classes** <term>Classen: regional assemblies of ministers and elders</term>, and **Synods** <term>Synoden: the highest governing assemblies of the Dutch Reformed Church</term> have been busy here and there defining and pruning those gatherings in such a way that they would almost all vanish into thin air and disappear if those rules were to remain and always apply, and if one were to carry out the threatened punishments according to them. Well, since matters stand thus,

# To the Reader.

<meta>catchword: Thus</meta>

Thus we are bound all the more to attend and maintain those private gatherings of the faithful, being fully assured that those who oppose and rail against these assemblies strive, in fact, against Christ and against his laws, and against obedience to his commands—as is clearly to be inferred from the reasons set forth above. Let us give no blind obedience here to human commands, whether they be ecclesiastical or political. The very learned and godly Master **Gisbertus Voetius** <term>Gisbertus Voetius (1589–1676): A hugely influential Dutch Reformed theologian and lead figure of the "Further Reformation," known for emphasizing personal piety alongside strict doctrine</term>, presenting to himself that objection:

*"These are Conventicles; consequently they are forbidden and unlawful,"*

answered to this:

*Suppose that those mutual visits of godliness—those gatherings and conversations—were forbidden somewhere; it would not follow from that that they are unlawful in the Court of Heaven or of the Conscience. For such a penal law would no more bind the consciences of the believers than the Ordinances of **Darius** <note>original: "Dan. 6"; refers to the biblical story of Daniel continuing to pray despite the King's decree</note>, and of the Great Council <note>original: "Hand. 4. en 5."; refers to Acts of the Apostles chapters 4 and 5, where the Apostles tell the authorities they must obey God rather than men</note>, and of the Roman Emperors against the Christians, and of **Charles the Fifth** and **Philip the Second** <note>The Spanish monarchs who ruled the Netherlands and persecuted Protestants during the 16th century</note> in the year 1563, and of other Princes against the Reformed.*

We must show through our actions that we desire to obey the command of God in this matter, and follow the counsel and the example of the pious, to the shaming of the world and the stopping of the mouths of the slanderers. A pious **Teacher** in the Netherlands wrote not long ago concerning this:

*Those meetings of the godly, for mutual exhortation, instruction, and comfort, were instituted by the Lord as auxiliary means to further our inward service which we owe to Him; they have always been praised by the Saints and practiced at all times. Thus it demonstrates itself that it is not only lawful, but also highly commendable to arrange the same occasionally, and to attend them with a humble and studious heart.*

---

Observe for a moment the zeal of the "praiseworthy" children of the world: how they flock together in great troops in taverns, drawing rooms, theaters, and such, to feed their lusts there, to destroy body and soul, and to give themselves over to all kinds of vanities—frequently mocking God, His Word, and His Ministers.

mock: and why should a studious and salvation-seeking Christian, seeing here or there a small company of pious people together, not be allowed to join them, in order to glorify God, to build up his own soul, and to offer his neighbor a word of edification? Unless we thought and knew that the Devil and his seed are always the same, and the world remains steeped in evil, it might seem strange to us that nominal Christians <note>Those who are Christian in name only, rather than in true practice or spirit.</note> still display the same wicked nature and slanderous spirit against the Holy Gatherings that the Pagans practiced against the Christians 1,400 or 1,500 years ago; for which reason we wish to shame them and silence their pagan chattering—not only by following the practice of the brave and zealous early Christians, but also with their own words, through the mouth of a Church Father <term>Church Father: An early Christian leader and writer from the centuries immediately following the Apostles, whose teachings are considered foundational.</term> who lived at that time and wrote an *Apology* <note>An "Apology" (from the Greek 'apologia') is a formal written defense of a belief or position, common in the early Church to defend the faith against Roman accusations.</note> for them.

*Tertullian* <note>original: "Tertullianus"; an influential early Christian author (c. 155 – c. 220 AD) from Carthage.</note> showed that the gatherings of Christians were not to be counted among illicit assemblies, in these words:

*This gathering of Christians is indeed rightly unlawful if it is like unlawful things; it is rightly to be condemned if anyone complains of it on the same grounds that factions <note>The author uses "factiën" (factions) to refer to groups organized for political dissent or illegal plotting.</note> or conspiracies are complained of. To whose destruction have we ever met? We are the same whether we are gathered or dispersed, whether we are together or alone; harming no one, grieving no one. When the upright and the good meet, when the pious and the chaste gather, it is not to be called a faction, but a court.* <note>original Latin: "Hæc coitio Christianorum merito sanè illicita, si illicitis par, meritò damnanda, si quis de eâ, queritur eo titulo, quo de factionibus querela est. In cujus perniciem aliquando convenimus? Hoc sumus congregati, quod & dispersi, hoc universi, quod & singuli; neminem lædentes, neminem contristantes, quum probi, quum boni, coëunt, quum pii quum casti congregantur, non est factio dicenda sed curia."</note>

These gatherings, he says, of the Christians were rightly to be considered unlawful if they were like illicit meetings; they were rightly to be condemned if they could be accused under the name and heading of Factions or conspiracies; but to whose ruin have we ever come together? We are the same, whether we are gathered or scattered, whether we are together or alone; we wrong no one, we grieve no one. Whenever the Pious and Good come together, whenever the Godly and Chaste assemble, then it must not be called a conspiracy, but a Council or a Court.

# To the Reader.

...and does other things that would require a whole book to recount them all; indeed, a book about this has appeared in German, in which a portion of these abuses are truthfully recounted, and it is worthy of being read, titled the *History of the Churches of Scotland*, and the terrible oppressions that have befallen them <note>The author is likely referring to a contemporary account of the "Killing Time," a period of intense conflict between the Covenanter movement and the forces of King Charles II and James VII.</note>. However, to give an example from which you may judge, I shall recount to you the contents of a Proclamation or Edict <note>original: "Placcaat"; an official decree or public notice.</note>, which the King recently issued in Edinburgh on March 1st in the year 1676 against the *Conventicles* <term>Conventicles: illegal religious meetings held by those who did not conform to the established Church of England or Church of Scotland.</term>; that is what holy Gatherings are called there, and they understand by them Gatherings and assemblies of some Believers for holy Exercises and Religious meditations, whether in the presence of a Teacher <note>In this context, a "Teacher" (Leeraar) refers to an ordained minister or preacher.</note>, or without one, whether in houses or in the fields.

In that Edict it is commanded:

1. That the Officers should vigorously execute the laws against the *Conventicles*; which mandate that they should imprison all who are found in those Assemblies; and demand heavy fines, or inflict corporal punishment, and especially the Teachers and those who favor them, with the highest punishments with which Rebels <note>original: "Oproer-makers"; literally, "insurrectionists" or "rioters."</note> are usually punished.

2. That they should apprehend and catch those who are by name declared, or should still be declared, as fugitive or rebel, and regarding whom it is forbidden to all that they should speak to them, greet them, lodge them, or do anything similar which might appear as a sign of politeness, humanity, or fellowship. Now, these are several hundred People (and that number is still increased daily), both Teachers and Citizens, and Inhabitants, Nobles and Commoners, who have ever been in holy though forbidden Assemblies and have been denounced; but being summoned, they dared not appear, fearing the punishments and seeing how others were treated.

3. That they should chase out and drive away from the Cities and Villages and Dwellings, the Households of those who [are] thus fugitive...

That all Owners, on whose Land or in whose House any *Conventicle* shall be held, shall be punished with a fine of one year's Rent or Lease of that Land or House, and this as often as they shall be held, without the Owner being allowed to recover it from the Tenant or Renter. 8. That the Edicts <note>original: "Plakkaaten"; official public decrees or laws.</note> against all Marriages and Baptisms of Children outside the public Churches should be strictly executed. 9. That as encouragement for the Informers, a good portion of the fines of those found guilty should be given to them for their service. 10. That no one should be allowed to keep in their house a Schoolmaster, Pedagogue, or other Person who would conduct Household Worship, who did not have a license

Nevertheless, thanks be to the Lord, the fire of zeal ignites more and more; the persecution is oil on the fire, the hunger for the Word becomes sharper, and the faithful Ministers <term>Ministers: or 'Leeraars' (teachers), referring to the Presbyterian clergy who refused to accept the King's authority over the church.</term>, who have been driven from their places (of which there are several hundred in that land), do not refrain from preaching the Gospel <note>original: "Euangelium"</note> to the hungry in houses and on fields and mountains, at the peril of their lives (so strictly is it forbidden to them); and the Lord works powerfully alongside them. ## To the Reader.

...suffering persecution, being cast out by force by the High Powers of this Land from my congregation, because of my opposition to the Service of the Liturgy <term>Formulier-dienst: The mandatory use of prescribed liturgical forms or prayer books, which some reformed ministers resisted as an infringement on spiritual freedom.</term> and the observance of Feast Days, and the usurping of ecclesiastical power by the Civil Magistrates <term>Politijken: Secular authorities or 'politicians' who claimed jurisdiction over church affairs, a major point of contention during the Dutch Reformation.</term>; and being hindered by the Ministers, my fellow-servants, from publicly preaching the Gospel, which they well know is entrusted to me as much as to them. Indeed, the height of the perversity of the Ministers goes so far in some places that they even dare to use the secular Arm <note>original: "wereltlijken Arm"; this refers to the use of state police or military force to enforce religious compliance.</note> to violently prevent that preaching of the Gospel by one who they must admit has a calling and commission from Christ for that purpose. How they shall answer for that before the Lord Jesus, I leave for you to consider; and at the same time, to judge from this how fallen and miserable the state of the Church of the Netherlands is, when even those who have a reputation for being alive and well-meaning are so far led astray.

But upon you, *my very beloved and dear Friends*, sanctified in the Congregation of *Sluis in Flanders*, I have had my eye most especially in bringing this book to light. For your edification has been particularly laid upon me, and you lie very near to my heart because of the good that the Lord has done for you under my ministry. I have indeed been separated from you by force, but I cannot, I may not rest from bringing you all possible help according to my ability and opportunity. Therefore, I send you this excellent book. I know you expect something of my own making, but as I am certain that I could not make it as well as this, I now give this to you much rather; and you may receive it with no less affection. I have preached to you much about these and similar matters, as you will remember, and have tried to teach you, publicly and privately, how Christ is to be used through faith in all necessities—which I also, by the Lord’s blessing, have found that many have grasped so well that they have successfully overcome many obstacles and difficulties, which the

<meta>catchword: comfort</meta>

<summary>The author describes his forced exile from his pulpit in Sluis due to his refusal to follow state-mandated liturgical forms. He dedicates this book to his former congregation as a source of spiritual guidance while he is forbidden from preaching to them in person.</summary>

## To the Reader:

...tend to hold back the comfort and the strength of Christians for a long time; this book shall be of further help to you in this, for which I pray to the Lord, and shall continue to pray. You all now miss my ministry for a time, and you suffer this violence along with me and sigh over it; but the Lord hears it, and sees it, and shall answer in His own time and way. Yet I assure you that, just as God is mighty to make all Grace <term>Genade: The unmerited favor of God, a central concept in Reformed theology where salvation is a gift rather than something earned.</term> abound in you, so that you, having all sufficiency in all things at all times, may abound in every good work, He shall likewise faithfully satisfy your souls with good things, even with the fatness of His house; for He is faithful who has promised, provided that you, in the evangelical way, by virtue of the Covenant of Grace <term>Verbondt der genade: The theological framework describing God's promise of eternal life through faith in Christ, contrasting with a 'Covenant of Works'.</term>, through faith, draw all comfort and strength from the Mediator <term>Middelaar: A title for Jesus Christ, acting as the legal and spiritual intermediary between God and humanity.</term>. Go to the Lord Jesus continually in the boldness of faith, and that according to the guidance that this *Author* prescribes to you according to the Word; and it shall be given to you according to the riches of God's glory (being truly a great measure) to be strengthened with power through His Spirit in the inner man, and the lack of this weak instrument <note>The "weak instrument" is a humble self-reference by the author to his own role as their minister.</note> shall be made up to you with abundant good. Now I find that when I begin to speak to you, my heart is very ready to write many things for your exhortation and warning; yet because I have perhaps already overstepped the bounds of a preface, I must restrain myself and postpone that until another occasion. *I shall mention only two things* about which I now wish to exhort you. *The first is, that you*, in your receiving of Christ as your Lord and King, might act so wisely that you do not fall short of the evangelical design of our God in this—that is, that you look upon and embrace Jesus' Kingly Office <term>Koninklijk Ampt: Christ's role as the sovereign ruler over the believer's life and the church, providing protection and governance.</term> as much for healing as you do His Priestly Office <term>Priester-Ampt: Christ's role in offering Himself as a sacrifice for sins and interceding with God on behalf of believers.</term>. It is a mistake that many Christians so receive Jesus as Lord and King that they only consent to Him in order to serve and obey Him, so that they only think that Jesus as King is received...

<summary>The author offers pastoral comfort to his displaced congregation, encouraging them to find spiritual nourishment in the "Covenant of Grace" and to correctly understand Christ's dual roles as King and Priest.</summary>
<keywords>Covenant of Grace, Mediator, Kingly Office, Priestly Office, Sluis, pastoral encouragement, inner man, grace</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's pastoral address, moving from a general encouragement to a specific theological correction regarding how the reader should view the Kingship of Jesus Christ. ### To the Reader.

...must [offer him] honor and obedience, as being the Lawgiver and commander, who is worthy—on account of His work as High Priest—that we dedicate all the powers of our soul to him, and devote everything to his service. Yet certainly this is a great and harmful misunderstanding; it is as if Jesus had only become King for himself and for his own honor, and not for the good of his dearly-bought children. Or, if one takes the idea a bit further, it is as if He, as King, only had to protect his subjects against the Devil and the world, and thereafter receive from them obedience out of gratitude for the redemption obtained through his Blood. 

But this is a legalistic <term>Wettisch: Referring to a 'Covenant of Works' mindset where one tries to earn God’s favor through law-keeping, rather than receiving it through faith.</term> conception of Christ’s Kingly Office <term>Koninklijk Ampt: One of the three roles of Christ (Prophet, Priest, and King) in Reformed theology; here specifically focusing on His power to rule over the believer's heart.</term>, through which we act in a legalistic and slavish manner—as if we were outside a Covenant of Grace <term>Verbondt der Genade: God’s promise of salvation through faith in Christ’s merits rather than the believer's own efforts.</term> with him in this matter. In such a state, we often find the heart disinclined to accept him under that consideration, because we find ourselves very unwilling and unable to obey his holy commands. Indeed, we find the heart rebellious, averse, loathing, and ready to run away from the Lord Jesus. Thus, we not only derive no comfort from our acceptance of him as King, but we receive from it a great wound and a shaking of our faith and trust. For we must accept Christ very willingly in his three Offices <note>The "three offices" (Latin: *munus triplex*) are Prophet, Priest, and King.</note>, and when this does not align with our desires—as is truly the case with a hypocrite—we are then ready to call all our graces and our entire spiritual state into doubt. 

That mistake you must then correct, and you shall have much comfort and refreshment from it, and Christ shall have all the more honor from it. Namely: you must accept him as King in such a way that you permit him to effectively, powerfully, and with a spiritual and lovely violence <note>Original: "lieffelijk gewelt." A common phrase in 17th-century Dutch piety referring to the "sweet" way God’s grace overpowers a rebellious human will without coercing it against its ultimate interest.</note> install himself as King. This should happen not only in your first turning to him, but continually in the exercise of your faith regarding him. You must take him into your heart as one who is both coming and willing to subject your heart and inclinations to himself, and to kill and break the spiritual enemies within your soul—yes, in order to...

<meta>catchword: to</meta>

<summary>The author warns against a "legalistic" view of Christ's kingship that focuses only on burdensome obedience, urging readers instead to view Christ as a King who graciously overpowers the heart's rebellion.</summary>
<keywords>Kingly Office, Covenant of Grace, legalism, spiritual enemies, obedience, heart rebellion, three offices of Christ</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Kingly Office of Christ, shifting from the danger of viewing it legalistically to the comfort of seeing Christ's power as the remedy for the internal rebellion and remaining sin within the believer's soul.</meta>

# To the Reader.

[...but] to root out your enmity, which you still have against him because of the remnants of sin, through his Spirit; as he was made King for that purpose in grace over us, to kill the foreign lords <note>The "foreign lords" refers to sinful desires, passions, or spiritual idols that rule the human heart in place of God.</note> within us, and as an all-conquering and overpowering King, to make everything subject and obedient to himself; as a King, he comes to heal the breach on our side, to bend our hearts toward willing obedience and the practical execution of his will; and in this respect, for a sincere Soul—however weak it may be, and however much it finds the **body of sin** <note>original: "Lichaam der zonde." A biblical term (Romans 6:6) referring to the corrupt human nature that persists even after a person begins their spiritual life.</note> stirring within itself—Christ’s **Kingly Office** shall be as dear and desirable and worthy of longing as his **Priestly Office**, and that soul will shrink as little from embracing Jesus as King as it shrinks from embracing him as Priest; and surely this is a most precious thought, which is of great value to us; especially to answer our objections that flow from the power of sin remaining within us.

We find ourselves greatly refreshed when many objections occur to us, arising from our heavy debt of sin, in that we can look to Jesus as a **High Priest**, who through his bloody atoning sacrifice <note>original: "zoen-offer." This refers to the propitiation or satisfaction of God's justice through the death of Christ, which removes the legal "debt" of sin.</note> (all-sufficient and infinitely worthy for the removal of the debt of sin) has made peace and **reconciliation**, and that we may freely—indeed, must in every way—accept him, as it is commanded of us; but concerning the *power* of sin within us, we find more difficulty. Yet the Lord be praised, that has been provided for just as well as the removal of the guilt; for we may and must look to Jesus as a King and exalted Prince, who can, will, and shall—and by virtue of his office <term>Ampt: A formal duty or role (Prophet, Priest, or King) that Christ is divinely commissioned by God to fulfill for the sake of the church.</term> must—subdue and conquer our and his spiritual enemies in us and for us, and we only have to consent to him and call upon him as such a King, given to us by the Father, and placed upon the throne to carry this out; Christ sits on his Priestly Throne to make reconciliation on God’s side, and on his Kingly throne, to [all...]

<summary>The author explains that Christ's role as King is not a burden but a comfort, as His royal power is used to heal the believer's heart and conquer the internal power of sin. He argues that the Kingly Office is just as necessary and desirable as the Priestly Office, which addresses the guilt of sin.</summary>
<keywords>Kingly Office, Priestly Office, Jesus Christ, sin, reconciliation, atonement, spiritual enemies, grace, sanctification</keywords>

# To the Reader.

...what is in us to bow to his willing obedience, which the Prophet expresses thus: *Behold, a man whose name is the Branch; he shall build the Temple of the Lord, and he shall bear the glory, and he shall sit and rule upon his throne, and he shall be a Priest upon his throne, Zech. 6:12, 13.* <note>The "Branch" (Dutch: *Spruite*) is a Messianic title from the Old Testament, representing Christ as the descendant of David who restores the temple.</note> The Lord Jesus is exalted as a Prince, to give repentance and forgiveness, *Acts 5: verse 31.* He has come to powerfully break the works of the Devil in us, *1 John 3: verse 8,* and to draw us out of this present evil world, *Gal. 1: verse 4,* and to redeem us from our vain conduct <note>original: "ydele vvandeling." This refers to an empty or godless way of life inherited from previous generations.</note>, *1 Pet. 1: verse 18,* and to save us from our sins, *Matt. 1: v. 21.* He, as King, would make us walk in his statutes, *Ezek. 36: verse 27,* and subdue and suppress our sins, *Micah 7: verse 19 <note>The original cites verse 16, but the text "subdue our sins" corresponds to Micah 7:19.</note>,* working all our works in us and for us, *Isa. 26: verse 12,* since it is he who works the willing and the doing according to his good pleasure, so that we may thus work out our salvation in fear and trembling, *Phil. 2: verse 12, 13.* He must and shall make us zealous for good works, *Tit. 2: v. 14,* and powerfully incline our heart, *Psalm 119: verse 35,* and draw us after him, *Song of Sol. 1: verse 4,* and purify our heart through faith, *Acts 15: v. 9.* Therefore, consent to him, that he may carry out his Kingly Office within you.

Secondly, regarding the Gatherings <note>original: "t'Samenkomften." These refers to *conventicles* or *gezelschappen*—private small-group meetings for prayer and Bible study that were central to the Dutch "Further Reformation" (Nadere Reformatie).</note>, which I have recommended at length above, and whose lawfulness, usefulness, and necessity I have proven; I would have you maintain them frequently, as I know that you do; yet without contempt or neglect of the public assemblies <note>Refers to the official Sunday services of the Reformed Church.</note>, concerning which you know that I have always warned you. Thus I shall end, praying, as Paul did for those of Colossae, that you might *be filled with the knowledge of God's will, in all wisdom and spiritual understanding, so that you may walk worthily of the Lord, unto all pleasing, being fruitful in every good work, and increasing in the knowledge of God; strengthened with all might, according to his glorious power, unto all patience and longsuffering with joy.*

<meta>catchword: then-</meta>

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, the author explains how Christ’s role as King empowers believers to overcome sin and perform good works. He also encourages the readers to continue their private spiritual meetings while remaining faithful to the public church.</summary>

<meta>This page begins the main devotional content of the book following Jacobus Koelman's preface. # JESUS All in All.

->Tune: *Our Father.*<- <note>original: "Stemme. Onze Vader." Referring to the standard melody used for the Lord's Prayer in Reformed liturgy.</note>

1. O Jesus, make the delusion of being "something"
In me, completely and utterly, into nothing.
And be alone henceforth *All in All,*
What I am, and what I shall be.
Let all that is old pass away,
And make *everything new* in me.

2. What does it benefit me, if I know everything,
If I am ignorant of you?
What does it harm me, if I knew nothing,
If only I know you, o *Christ!*
O only pledge worthy of being known;
Be *All in All my understanding.*

3. The <term>Triangle</term> <note>A common 17th-century emblem: the human heart was depicted as a triangle (representing the Trinity or the heart's three corners) which cannot be filled by the "circle" of the world, but only by God.</note> of my eager heart,
Is never filled by the <term>all-encompassing world.</term> <note>original: "’t All-rondt," referring to the physical globe or the circularity of worldly vanities.</note>
My hope, and fear, and joy, and desire,
And love never find rest.
Be *All*, Lord, as the occasion requires,
In *all my affections.*

4. As the man is, so is his strength: <note>A reference to Judges 8:21.</note>
But I am nothing, and my power is nothing.
My enemies are many and evil,
Their schemes are strong, their strength is hollow.
So that I do not come to a fall,
Be, Lord, *my entire suit of armor.* <note>original: "mijn Wapen-rusting All," referencing the "Armor of God" from Ephesians 6.</note>

5. Away with profit, pleasure, status, and honor,
I want you as my only goal;
Whether I eat, or whether I drink,
Or whether I do any other thing;
All that I think, or speak, or work,
Be *All*, Lord, in my focus.

6. The friendship of this world is evil,
In enmity and hatred against God,
So that I henceforth am no friend of it;
Nor know Father or Mother,
Except those who are also of your number;
Be, Lord, in all my friendship, all.

<meta>catchword: 7. Not</meta>

<summary>The opening hymn of the collection, "JESUS All in All," calls for the total displacement of the self and worldly desires by the presence of Christ. It utilizes 17th-century metaphors, such as the "triangle heart," to illustrate that only the divine can satisfy human longing.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, All in All, devotional poetry, Further Reformation, triangle heart emblem, spiritual armor, Our Father tune, self-denial</keywords>

<sig>4.</sig>

7. Not unto me, not unto me, o dear Lord, <note>A paraphrase of Psalm 115:1, "Not unto us, O Lord, not unto us, but unto thy name give glory."</note>
But to Your Name alone give the glory,
For my righteousness before God;
From the beginning, the progress, and the end,
Of the holiness that I pledge,
Be *All*, Lord, in all my faith.

8. Be to me in all prosperity *All*,
And *All* in every misfortune,
Be to me also *Everything* in every place,
And *Everything* at all times as well;
Whether I live, or shall die,
Be, Lord, and remain my *All in All.* <note>The phrase "All in All" (original: "Al in All") is a recurring motif in 17th-century Dutch devotional literature, referencing 1 Corinthians 15:28 and emphasizing Christ's total sufficiency.</note>
