<meta>This page continues the theological argument that the believer's victory over sin is not achieved through their own strength, but through their legal and spiritual union with Christ. It transitions from viewing Christ’s death as a historical event to applying its power as a legal liberation from the "tyranny" of sin.</meta>

-> ### for the killing of sins. ### <-

...recounted—yea, more than a conqueror through him who loved him, *Rom. 8: verse 37*. Now, faith thus working upon **Christ**, as a **Public Person** <note>Theological term for a representative, such as a king or legal proxy, whose actions are legally credited to those he represents.</note> dying and overcoming **Death** and sin, allows the believer not only to conclude the certainty of the victory—knowing that our **old Man** <note>A biblical term referring to the corrupt, unregenerate nature of a human being.</note> is crucified with **Christ**, *Rom. 6: verse 6*—but he may also draw strength from **Christ's** cross to stand and to fight against the stirrings of this conquered and slain enemy. Those who are **Christ's** have crucified the flesh with its passions and desires, *Gal. 5: verse 24*. But how? To wit, through the cross of **Christ**; for by it the **World** is crucified to me, says the **Apostle**, *Gal. 6: verse 14*, and I to the **World**; our **old Man** is crucified with **Christ**, so that the body of sin might be brought to nothing, *Rom. 6: verse 6*.

**Thirteenthly.** The believer, being truly **Dead** to sin through the cross of **Christ**, must regard himself as legally freed from that yoke of bondage under sin and **Death**. The law rules over a **Man** as long as he lives, *Rom. 7: verse 1*; but through the body of **Christ**, believers are dead to the law, *verse 4*. That law of sin and of death which rules over a **Man** who still lives in nature—and is not yet planted by faith into the likeness of His death, nor buried with Him through **Baptism** into death, *Rom. 6: verse 4, 5*—has no dominion over the believer such as it once had. For the law of the **Spirit** of life in **Christ Jesus** has made her free from the law of sin and of death, *Rom. 8: verse 2*, so that the believer is now free from that tyranny. That tyrant can now exercise no legal jurisdiction or authority over him, and therefore he may repel the insolence of that tyrant with even greater courage—one who, against all right and fairness, still seeks to hold dominion over him. He is no legal subject of that cruel and raging **Prince**, or of that spiritual wickedness.

<meta>This page continues the legal and marital metaphor established in the previous section. It argues that because the believer is legally "dead" to the law of sin and "remarried" to Christ, any subsequent urge to sin is an illegal act of treason against their new Sovereign.</meta>

-> Christ the Way, <-

**Fourteenthly.** The believer, then, rejecting that **jurisdiction** <note>The legal authority or rule of sin.</note> under which he previously lived, and being now under a new **Husband** <note>The Dutch word 'Man' here refers to the marital metaphor in Romans 7, where the soul is 'married' to Christ.</note> and under a new **Law**, namely the **Law** of the **Spirit** of life in **Christ Jesus**, must regard all the stirrings of sin as unlawful and as the treacherous acts of a **tyrant**. Since the **old Man** <note>The corrupt human nature before spiritual rebirth.</note> is crucified with **Christ** so that the body of sin might be brought to nothing, the believer must no longer serve sin, *Rom. 6: verse 6*. And being now dead, he is freed from sin, *verse 7*, and married to another, namely to Him who is risen from the dead; thus he should not serve sin, but bear fruit for God, *Rom. 7: verse 4*. Consequently, he must view all the stirrings of the flesh and all the inclinations and movements of the old **Law** of sin as acts of treason and rebellion against the right and **jurisdiction** of the believer’s new **Lord** and **Husband**. He is therefore bound to seize this **old Man**, this body of death, and all its members, as traitors against the rightful **King** and **Husband**, and to take them captive to the **King**, so that He may give and execute judgment against them as enemies of His **Kingdom** and of His **interest** <note>His rightful claim or stake</note> in the soul. Since they are no longer servants of sin but of righteousness, *Rom. 6: verse 18*, they ought no longer to present their members to be servants of impurity and of unrighteousness leading to more unrighteousness, *verse 19*. And since they are no longer debtors to the flesh to live according to the flesh, they must kill the deeds of the body through the **Spirit**, *Rom. 8: 12, 13*, and crucify the flesh with its stirrings and desires, *Gal. 5: verse 24*. That is to say: by bringing them to the cross of **Christ**, where they were first condemned and crucified in their full body and strength, so that a new judgment, as it were, might go out against them as members of that condemned **Tyrant** and as belonging to that crucified body.

## Warnings.

For the further clarification of what has been said / I wish to propose a few particulars for warning and guidance / namely:

Firstly: This work of laying the burden of this matter upon *Christ* through faith / must be handled with great simplicity of heart / aiming for the glory of God / and the advancement of his work in the soul / and not for self-ends / and fleshly side-interests <note>original: "by-respecten" - secondary motives or personal gain</note> / lest we ruin everything thereby.

**Ninthly.** In following this course, we should not think that we will always have good speed at the first. ### On the Mortification of Sin

corruption <note>The Dutch "verdorventheyd" refers to the theological concept of Total Depravity or the "old man" within a believer.</note>, which sometimes had the upper hand. Just as Peter was free from a public and direct denial of his Master, nevertheless, that same corruption stirred itself thereafter—though not so violently that it led him to such a height of sin—yet so far that it made him do that which was partly a denial of his Master, as when Paul withstood him to his face <note>A reference to the "Incident at Antioch" in Galatians 2:11–12, where Paul rebuked Peter for refusing to eat with Gentile Christians.</note>, because he was to be blamed for the reason that he withdrew himself from the Gentiles out of fear of those of the Circumcision, etc. Galatians 2: verses 11, 12.

Likewise, even if a particular lust might be so far brought under by grace that a person did not find it so violent for a significant time as it was before, he still cannot say that it is entirely killed, because it can stir itself hereafter in a weaker degree. Yea, he cannot say whether that same corruption will not arise just as violently as ever before he comes to die, and whether Satan might not think to enter through that same breach in the soul through which he once entered. Indeed, who can say whether God will not permit that corruption, which lay as dead for a long time, to become alive again for a season, and for a time drive the soul as violently as ever, and for a time have the upper hand again? And this should teach all believers to be sober <note>In this context, "sober" means spiritually alert and serious-minded rather than just avoiding alcohol.</note>, watchful, and to walk in fear, and to have a vigilant eye even on such lusts and fleshly movements of which they think they have obtained the victory.

**Thirteenthly.** We must not think that we are not gaining ground against corruption because we constantly notice that it stirs more or less: for just as corruption is not always strongest (as we said above) nor has deeper entrance into the soul when its movements and stirrings are most felt; so we must also not think that nothing has been gained over a lust <term>Begeerlijkheyd: Often translated as "concupiscence," it refers to a strong, disordered desire or longing for something sinful.</term> because we are still troubled and tormented by its movements. For it is a great advantage to be more sensitive to the movements of this enemy, and

### On the Mortification of Sin.

...-tion <note>The text begins with the suffix "-heyd," completing the word "verdorventheyd" (corruption) from the previous page.</note> which sometimes had the upper hand; just as Peter was free from an open and direct denial of his Master, yet that same corruption stirred within him afterward—though not so violently that it led him to such a height of sin. ### Christ the Way,

...our more faithful and more active wrestling against the same <note>referring to "corruption" or "lust" from the previous page</note> can make its slightest stirrings more sensitive to our feelings; just as one may notice more the movements and disturbances that a **wrongdoer** makes while he is in custody and in the **prison-house**, than his greater rages before he was imprisoned; nevertheless, he may certainly be in chains despite all this.

**Fourteenthly.** Even if we, by following this path, find no present rest and deliverance, we should still value it highly if the Lord helps us to stand, after we have done what we can, even if we do not obtain the hoped-for outcome; if He gives us grace to remain without becoming weary or fainting, and to be firmly **resolved** never to give up, then we have reason to praise Him; if we are kept constantly in pursuit of the **Enemy**, that is to our great advantage: the victory will come in **God’s** time. If our resistance continues so long that we are resolved never to take or give **quarter** <note>original: "quartier"; a military term for showing mercy or granting a truce to an enemy</note>, though our torment and **trial** should be the greater, and our comfort and rest the less, we ought to praise Him, and rejoice in hope for what He will yet do for us: for He who shall come will come, and will not tarry <note>A reference to Hebrews 10:37, promising that God’s intervention is certain</note>; let us wait for Him while doing our duty, and faithfully holding our post.

**Fifteenthly.** Even if we obtain some rest or silence from the violence of **raging lusts** for a little time, and we are not continually driven and carried away by them, we ought to be thankful for that, and walk humbly before Him, so that He is not provoked by our ingratitude and pride to let those **furious dogs** loose upon us again.

<meta>This page continues the author's practical advice on the "mortification" (the spiritual process of killing off sinful impulses). It concludes the fifteenth point regarding the tactical deceptions of the enemy and begins a new section addressing common doubts and objections from believers who feel their struggle is getting harder rather than easier.</meta>

->concerning the killing <note>mortification</note> of sin.<-

...here sound a false alarm, and immediately plan an attack in another place, through some other corrupted inclination. Oh, how much we need spiritual wisdom, so that we may have better knowledge of his snares and deceptions! Let us then so fight against one limb of this **body of death** <note>original: "lichaam des doods"; a biblical term for the sinful nature</note>, that we also keep our eye on the others, so that **Satan** gains no entry through the back door while we intend to keep him out at the front door; he can make use of extremes, and play his game with both—yes, and even win his point—if we do not pay attention to it.

## Objections answered.

It will not be in vain, for the further opening of the matter, to remove a scruple <note>a "scrupel" here refers to a lingering doubt or a troubled conscience</note> or two. Some might say that they cannot notice that all their trouble in this has any good outcome: for never did they find their **corruption** stir itself more, or work more vigorously and constantly, than since they began in earnest to fight against it; so that this would seem not to be the right way.

I Answer: Although a solution to this doubt can be obtained from what was said before, particularly from the ninth and thirteenth warnings, I will nevertheless present these things for further clarification of the matter.

1. Could not much of this flow from the fact that you do not take the whole work away from yourself and lay it upon **Christ**, as you ought to do? Test yourself, and see.

2. Can the **Devil** not rage the most when he thinks he will shortly be cast out? Can he not labor to cause the soul the most trouble when he sees that he will certainly be evicted from some of his strongholds?

3. Can the **Devil** not do this intentionally to drive the soul to despair of ever getting **corruption** brought under and killed; or to a faint—

<meta>catchword: ken/ (completes "bezwijken," meaning to faint, succumb, or give way)</meta>

<summary>The author warns that spiritual progress is often met with increased resistance from Satan, who uses tactical distractions and "back doors" to lead the soul astray. He addresses the concern that sin feels more active when one starts fighting it, suggesting this is a sign of the Devil's desperation rather than failure.</summary>

<keywords>mortification, body of death, spiritual warfare, Satan's tactics, Christ's help, spiritual wisdom, corruption, despair</keywords>

...that he has kept you so long out of hell / and considers it of great value / that he gives you the grace / to see and notice the movements of corruption <note>original: "verdorventheidt"; referring to the innate sinful nature</note> / which fleshly **Men** never notice / and that he helps you resist / and complain about the corruption / with which they sweetly unite themselves.

Through your hatred of sin, When all that **Satan** can do / cannot bring about / that you conspire with these desires <note>original: "begeerlijkheden"; refers to sinful cravings or lusts</note> / or otherwise sweetly embrace them / or lie down in peace with the decayed members of the **old Man** <note>original: "ouden Mensch"; a biblical term (Romans 6:6) for the unregenerate human nature</note> / as others do.

5. Through your watchfulness, When all your failures and stumblings make you watch more earnestly against that enemy.

6. Through your exercise of faith, When you are always carrying sin and its desires to **Christ** / to kill them / and bring them under / as believing the contents of the **Gospel** <note>original: "Euangelium"</note> / and the **New Covenant.**

7. Through your hope, Which appears through your not despairing / nor giving up on the matter as a hopeless work / nor turning aside to evil actions.

8. Through your prayer, When you constantly call to him for help / who alone can help.

9. Through your wrestling, And standing out against all opposition / for thereby his strength is perfected in weakness / *2 Corinthians 12:9.* <note>The author quotes the Latin context of the New Testament: "My grace is sufficient for thee: for my strength is made perfect in weakness."</note>

10. Through your obedience: For it is his command / that you stand / and fight this good fight of faith <note>original: "strijdt des geloofs"</note>.

So that if you have a desire / to glorify him / then no opportunity is lacking for you / to do it / even in this state / in which you complain / that you cannot get him glorified. And if these grounds do not satisfy you / it is to be feared / that it is not so much a desire to glorify him / that makes you cry so earnestly for immediate deliverance from the torment of the flesh / and from its desires / as it is something [else...]

<summary>The author lists seven additional ways a believer honors God during spiritual struggle, including through hatred of sin, watchfulness, faith, and prayer. He argues that even the struggle itself is a form of service that glorifies God.</summary>

But in the third place, there may be an objection raised; is it not promised / *That sin shall not have dominion over us, for we are not under the law, but under grace?* Romans 6: verse 14. <note>The author references the famous promise that grace frees a person from the tyranny of sin.</note> How can we then be anything / but constantly troubled / when we do not find this promise fulfilled?

I Answer. 1. Sin is not always victorious and reigning / when it seems most to rage and rave; your resistance against it / as you fight and wrestle against it / shows well / that it has no full dominion; as long as an invading usurper <note>original: "geweld-drijver"; one who rules by force rather than right</note> is resisted / he has no full dominion / because he does not have peaceful possession of what he seeks.

2. Victory / and a full victory over the flesh and its desires <note>original: "begeerlijkheden"; refers to the sinful cravings of human nature</note> is promised to no believer / at their first appearance in the field / to fight / nor is it given to all / in any measure / at their first putting on of their armor.

3. Therefore it is permitted for you / to fight on / and to wait for that victory / namely / that sin shall not have dominion over you; for it may come in its own time.

4. God has His own time and occasions / in which He fulfills His promises; and we must allow Him latitude <note>original: "wijdte"; scope or room to act</note> in respect of the time / when / and in respect of the certain "how" / and in respect of the degree / in which He will make good His promises.

Therefore / although the promise does not yet seem to be fulfilled / yet there is no true cause for disturbance of the mind / because it can and shall be fully fulfilled hereafter; and your wrestling against sin makes known / that it is already fulfilled in a great measure / because where sin has a full dominion / it suppresses all resistance or contradiction / except for some weak resistance / which a natural conscience <note>original: "conscientie"; the innate sense of right and wrong</note> / —

### unto growth in grace.

In response to this—although it is easily inferred from what was said in our previous discourse what might be said here—I shall nonetheless briefly remind the reader of these things, as they are useful here.

Firstly: The believer should consider what an adornment it is to the soul to wear this **New Man**, who is created according to **God’s Image**, *Ephesians 4: verse 23.* what excellence lies in regaining that lost glory, holiness, and the Image of God; and what a benefit the soul receives from it, when *she is made fit to share in the inheritance of the saints in light, Colossians 1: verse 12. and walks worthily of the Lord in all pleasingness, bearing fruit in every good work, and increasing in the knowledge of God, Colossians 1: verse 10. and is strengthened with all power, according to the might of His glory, unto all patience and long-suffering with joy, verse 11.* and when the abundance of the grace of the Spirit within us ensures that we shall never be idle nor unfruitful in the knowledge of our Lord Jesus Christ, *2 Peter 1: verse 8. and that we are vessels for honor, sanctified and fit for the Lord’s use, prepared for every good work, 2 Timothy 2: verse 21.* What glory and peace are found here, to be found obedient to the manifold commandments God has given to be holy? What danger there is in lacking holiness, since without it we cannot see God? *Hebrews 12: verse 14.* How inexcusable is it for our profession of faith—being members of such a holy Head <note>Christ is referred to as the "Head" of the Church, which is His body. The author argues it is contradictory for the "limbs" (believers) to be unholy if the "Head" (Christ) is holy.</note>—to be unholy? What profit, joy, and contentment there is in being Temples of the Holy Spirit, in walking according to the Spirit, in bringing forth fruits to the glory of the Father, etc. The consideration of these and other motivations for this practice of <term>sanctification: (Dutch: *heiligmaking*) the ongoing process by which a believer is made holy through the Holy Spirit</term> ought to arm the soul with a full resolution and harden it against all opposition.

Secondly: It should be remembered that this work, although it is laid upon us as our duty, and we...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on sanctification (the process of being made holy). ->Christ the Way,<-
118

to which we are called by God, yet it is beyond our hand and power. It is true: in conversion <term>bekeering: the spiritual turning point where a person is brought to faith</term> the seed of grace is cast into the soul, new habits <term>heblijkheden: from the Latin 'habitus,' referring to infused spiritual qualities or dispositions of the soul</term> are poured in, a new principle of life is given, and the heart of stone is changed into a heart of flesh; nonetheless, these principles and habits cannot work of themselves, nor be brought into activity by anything that a believer—considered in himself and without Divine help—can do. 

But this work of sanctification <term>heiligmaking: the ongoing process of being set apart and made holy</term> and of growth in grace must be carried forward by Divine help, through the Spirit of Jesus, dwelling and working within us; and therefore it is called **the sanctification of the Spirit**, *2 Thessalonians 2: verse 13, 1 Peter 1: verse 2.* The **God of peace** must sanctify us, *1 Thessalonians 5: verse 23.* We are said to be sanctified by **God the Father**, *Jude verse 1,* and by the **Holy Spirit**, *Romans 15: verse 16;* see also *1 Corinthians 6: verse 11.* 

We ought to remember that we can do nothing of ourselves, *2 Corinthians 3: verse 5,* and that He must work in us both **to will and to do** according to His good pleasure, *Philippians 2: verse 13.* Although no believer would wish to call this truth into doubt, nonetheless it shall perhaps be found, after examination, that a primary cause of their lack of growth in grace and of making no progress in this work is that they do not labor as those who believe this; rather, they undertake this work as if it were a work which they themselves could bring about and accomplish without special Divine help. Therefore, the believer should remain, live, and work in the faith of this truth.

**Thirdly.** Thus, in conducting this work, believers ought not to trust in their own strength, in the habits of grace, in their previous experiences <term>bevindingen: personal, experimental knowledge of God's work in the soul</term>, in their knowledge and abilities, or the like; neither should they trust in any outward means which they must observe, because the wisdom, power, and help which their case requires is not to be found in those things; nonetheless [they] must

they must not think / to set these means and duties <note>In this context, 'means' refers to 'means of grace' like prayer, reading scripture, and attending church; 'duties' refers to the moral and spiritual obligations of a Christian.</note> aside; for then they would sin against God / they would hinder themselves / from receiving the help / power / and influence / which God is accustomed to bring to the soul in and through the use of these means; and at the same time they would tempt God / by prescribing another way to him / than he has thought to take. The believer then ought to observe the prescribed means and duties / and that diligently / earnestly / and constantly / and yet he ought to lean so little upon them / and to expect so little help and power from them / as if he used them not at all; and truly this would be a right way / yes, the most advantageous and profitable way / of observing the duties / to be diligent in the use of the same / on account of God’s Command / and yet to place our hope and expectation on God alone / and to look beyond the institutions <note>The 'institutions' refer to the established religious practices or ordinances.</note> toward our help.

Fourthly. Although it is true that the power and grace of God alone begins / and continues this work of sanctification <term>heiligmakingh: the process of being made holy and more like Christ</term> in the soul / nonetheless / though he might conduct and complete this work in the soul (if only he saw it to be to his glory) without the intervention of second causes or means / so he has nevertheless thought it fit for the glory of his name / to work this work through means / and particularly by the believer setting himself to the work. He does not work in man / as if he were a stone or a block / but he uses him as a rational creature / endowed with a rational soul / having useful and necessary faculties / and having a body / made capable through the organs <term>werkt-tuigen: literally 'work-tools'; referring here to the physical senses and limbs as instruments of the soul</term> / to serve the soul in its deeds. Therefore the believer must not think to lie still / and do nothing : for he is commanded to work out his own salvation / and that because it is God / who works in him both to will and to work; because God works all / therefore he should work / so the Apostle reasons <note>A reference to Philippians 2:12-13.</note> ; so that God’s working is an argument / and a motive for man /

# Christ the Way, #

to work / and not an argument for him / to lie still / and do nothing. And here is the holy art and divine ability required in this work / namely / that the soul is as diligent and active / as if she could bring forth fruit in her own strength / and through her own operation / and yet is so detached from herself / from her own grace / ability / knowledge / and experience in her operation / as if she lay there but like a simple block <note>The "simple block" (Dutch: *enkel blok*) is a common 17th-century metaphor for spiritual passivity, comparing the human soul to a piece of wood or stone that cannot move unless acted upon by God.</note> / and only moved / as if moved by an external force.

Fifthly. The soul that wishes to make progress in Christianity / and grow in grace / ought to remember / that Christ is presented to us / as an example / which we must follow / and that we therefore must continually place Christ before our eyes / as our Pattern / so that we might follow his footsteps / 1 Pet. 2: verse 21. But one must at the same time remember / that he is not like other patterns or examples / which can help the Man / who follows them / in no other way than through their objective or visual contemplation <note>The author distinguishes between human examples, which we merely look at and copy, and Christ, whose "example" actually transmits spiritual power to the observer.</note>: For looking by faith upon this example / shall bring power to the Man / who strives to follow / whereby he shall be made able / to better follow his example. Sixthly. **Seventhly.** To this end, believers must lay themselves open to the <term>influences of Christ</term> <note>In 17th-century theology, "influences" referred to the flow of spiritual life and grace from Christ into the soul, much like how light "influences" the earth.</note> / and guard against placing obstacles in the way / by grieving the Spirit / through whom He conveys and shares those influences to the soul / and by doubting Christ's faithfulness and unchanging willingness / and by not believing / which, like a violent <term>humor</term> <note>In early modern medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids; a "violent humor" would be a blockage or an imbalance that prevents healthy circulation.</note> stops up the passage. Thus, believers must lay open / by looking / longing / and waiting / seeking / drawing and sucking from Him / that which they have need of / and by guarding themselves against everything / which can provoke the Lord to anger / whether in omission / or commission. Here is required a holy / humble / sober and watchful walk / an earnest / steady / and hungry looking toward Him / and a patient waiting for fulfillment / and supply from Him. This is to open the mouth wide / so that He may fill it / to lay oneself before the **Sun of Righteousness** <note>A reference to Malachi 4:2, symbolizing Christ as the source of light and spiritual health.</note> / so that its rays might shine upon her / and warm and enliven her / and to wait like a beggar at the King's gate / until He gives the alms.

The believer must then view these influences as dearly bought / by the Blood of Jesus Christ ; so that divine power gives us all things / that pertain to life and godliness, through the knowledge of him / who has called us to glory and virtue, 2 Pet. 1: v. 3. of the letter, but in the newness of the Spirit, *Rom. 7: verse 4, 6.* <note>The author is quoting Romans 7:6, contrasting the legalistic obedience to the "letter" of the Mosaic law with a spiritual life empowered by the Holy Spirit.</note> He is made alive as a head / and as the head has become alive / so the members <note>In 17th-century theology, "members" (ledemaaten) refers to individual believers who make up the "body" of the Church, of which Christ is the Head.</note> can in every way expect from the same some impartation of life / and live in the power of the life of the head, see *Col. 3: verse 1, 2.*
